# Joko Widodo

Joko Widodo

President of Indonesia from 2014 to 2024

In this [Indonesian name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_names#Naming_forms "Indonesian names"), there is no [family name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surname "Surname") or [patronymic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patronymic "Patronymic").

**Joko Widodo** (Indonesian:[\[ˌd͡ʒokowiˈdodo\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Malay "Help:IPA/Malay"); born **Mulyono**, Indonesian:[\[muˈljono\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Malay "Help:IPA/Malay"); 21 June 1961), often known [mononymously](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mononym "Mononym") as **Jokowi** (Indonesian:[\[d͡ʒoˈkowi\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/Malay "Help:IPA/Malay")), is an Indonesian politician and businessman who served as the seventh [president of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Indonesia "President of Indonesia") from 2014 to 2024. Previously a member of the [Indonesian Democratic Party of Struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_Democratic_Party_of_Struggle "Indonesian Democratic Party of Struggle") (PDI-P), he was the country's first president not to emerge from the political or military elite. Before becoming president, he served as mayor of [Surakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surakarta "Surakarta") from 2005 to 2012 and as [governor of Jakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Jakarta "Governor of Jakarta") from 2012 to 2014.

Born and raised in Surakarta, Widodo studied at [Gadjah Mada University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gadjah_Mada_University "Gadjah Mada University") and later worked in the furniture industry, including as an exporter, before entering politics. As mayor of Surakarta, he gained national attention for his populist style, emphasis on urban redevelopment, and direct engagement with residents through _blusukan_ visits. His tenure as governor of Jakarta further raised his national profile, with policies on healthcare, education, bureaucratic reform, flood mitigation, and public transport.

In the [2014 Indonesian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Indonesian_presidential_election "2014 Indonesian presidential election"), Widodo was nominated by the PDI-P and defeated [Prabowo Subianto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prabowo_Subianto "Prabowo Subianto"), before being re-elected in [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Indonesian_general_election "2019 Indonesian general election"). As president, he prioritised infrastructure development, social welfare expansion, bureaucratic reform, and economic development. His administration also pursued a pragmatic foreign policy centred on sovereignty, economic diplomacy, maritime affairs, and a more assertive response to issues such as illegal fishing and disputes in the [South China Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_China_Sea "South China Sea"). Other major initiatives of his presidency included the [planned relocation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_of_Indonesia#Relocation_to_Nusantara "Capital of Indonesia") of Indonesia's capital to [Nusantara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nusantara_(planned_city) "Nusantara (planned city)").

Widodo's presidency has drawn both praise and criticism. Supporters have credited him with [expanding infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Strategic_Projects "National Strategic Projects"), improving public services, and maintaining political popularity, while critics pointed to his use of the death penalty, restrictions on civil liberties, [weakened anti-corruption efforts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Indonesian_protests_and_riots "2019 Indonesian protests and riots"), environmental controversies, and signs of democratic backsliding. His final years in office were overshadowed by accusations of political interference and dynastic politics, especially after his son [Gibran Rakabuming Raka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gibran_Rakabuming_Raka "Gibran Rakabuming Raka") became Prabowo's running mate in the [presidential election of 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Indonesian_presidential_election "2024 Indonesian presidential election"). After leaving office that year, Widodo's falling out with the PDI-P culminated in his expulsion from the party, with Widodo joining the [Indonesian Solidarity Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_Solidarity_Party "Indonesian Solidarity Party") the following year.

## Early life and education

Joko Widodo was born as Mulyono at Brayat Minulya General Hospital in Surakarta on 21 June 1961.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Presiden-RI-JW-0)[\[3\]](#refnote-Presiden-RI-JW)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Name-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-Name)[&#8202;](#ref-back-5-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-5) Of Javanese heritage, he is the only son of [Widjiatno Notomihardjo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Widjiatno_Notomihardjo "Widjiatno Notomihardjo") and [Sudjiatmi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudjiatmi "Sudjiatmi") and the eldest of four siblings. His father came from [Karanganyar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karanganyar_Regency "Karanganyar Regency"), while his grandparents came from a village in [Boyolali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boyolali_Regency "Boyolali Regency").[&#8202;](#ref-back-6-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-6) His three younger sisters are Iit Sriyantini, [Idayati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idayati "Idayati"), and Titik Relawati.[&#8202;](#ref-back-7-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-7)[&#8202;](#ref-back-8-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-8) Because he was often sick as a toddler, his name was later changed—a common practice in [Javanese culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_culture "Javanese culture")—to Joko Widodo, with _widodo_ meaning "healthy" in [Javanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javanese_language "Javanese language").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Name-1)[\[4\]](#refnote-Name) At the age of 12, he began working in his father's furniture workshop.[&#8202;](#ref-back-9-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-9)[&#8202;](#ref-back-bantaran-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-bantaran) During his youth, his family lived in three different rented homes, one of which was later declared condemned by the government, an experience that strongly affected him. He later cited such experiences in developing low-income housing in [Surakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surakarta "Surakarta") during his mayoralty.[&#8202;](#ref-back-11-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-11)

Jokowi began his education at State Elementary School 111 Tirtoyoso, an ordinary public school.[&#8202;](#ref-back-12-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-12) He continued at State Junior High School 1 Surakarta.[&#8202;](#ref-back-13-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-13) He had hoped to attend State Senior High School 1 Surakarta, but his entrance-exam score was not high enough, and he instead enrolled at State Senior High School 6 in the city.[&#8202;](#ref-back-14-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-14) When he was in college, his [KKN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_study_service_(Indonesia) "Student study service (Indonesia)") buddies nicknamed him 'Jack'.[&#8202;](#ref-back-15-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-15)

## Business career

After graduating from university, Widodo began working at PT [Kertas Kraft Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kertas_Kraft_Aceh?action=edit&redlink=1 "Kertas Kraft Aceh")\[ [id](https://id.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kertas%20Kraft%20Aceh "id:Kertas Kraft Aceh")\] (KKA), a [state-owned company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State-owned_enterprise "State-owned enterprise") in [Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aceh "Aceh"), [Sumatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumatra "Sumatra").[&#8202;](#ref-back-ibunda-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-ibunda) Between 1986 and 1988, he worked in what is now [Bener Meriah Regency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bener_Meriah_Regency "Bener Meriah Regency") as a supervisor of forestry and raw materials at a [Sumatran pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinus_merkusii "Pinus merkusii") plantation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-17-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-17)[&#8202;](#ref-back-18-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-18) He soon lost interest in the company and returned home. He then worked in his grandfather's furniture factory for a year before establishing his own business, Rakabu, named after his first child. He raised his initial capital through a Rp 15 million contribution from his father and a bank loan. The company, which mainly produced [teak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teak "Teak") furniture, nearly went bankrupt at one point but survived after receiving an IDR 500 million loan from [Perusahaan Gas Negara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perusahaan_Gas_Negara "Perusahaan Gas Negara"). By 1991, it had begun exporting and found success in international markets. The company first entered the European market through France, and it was through a French client named Bernard who gave Joko Widodo the nickname "Jokowi".[&#8202;](#ref-back-ibunda-1)[\[16\]](#refnote-ibunda)[&#8202;](#ref-back-19-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-19)[&#8202;](#ref-back-rakabu-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-rakabu)

By 2002, Widodo had become chairman of Surakarta's furniture manufacturers association.[&#8202;](#ref-back-rakabu-1)[\[20\]](#refnote-rakabu) He eventually decided to enter politics and pursue reform in his hometown of Surakarta after observing the orderly layout of several European cities while promoting his furniture there.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bantaran-1)[\[10\]](#refnote-bantaran) After becoming mayor, he also entered a joint venture with politician and former [lieutenant general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_general "Lieutenant general") [Luhut Binsar Pandjaitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luhut_Binsar_Pandjaitan "Luhut Binsar Pandjaitan"), when the two founded PT Rakabu Sejahtera, combining Rakabu with Luhut's PT Toba Sejahtera.[&#8202;](#ref-back-21-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-21)[&#8202;](#ref-back-22-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-22)

In 2018, Jokowi reported a net worth of Rp 50.25 billion (US$3.5 million as of 2018), most of it in the form of property holdings in Central Java and Jakarta.[&#8202;](#ref-back-23-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-23)

## Early political career

### Mayor of Surakarta

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/82/Official_Portrait_of_Joko_Widodo_as_the_Mayor_of_Surakarta.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Official_Portrait_of_Joko_Widodo_as_the_Mayor_of_Surakarta.jpg) Official portrait of Joko Widodo as mayor of Surakarta, 2005[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Mayor_of_Surakarta_and_Vice_Mayor_of_Surakarta_in_wayang_costumes.jpg/500px-Mayor_of_Surakarta_and_Vice_Mayor_of_Surakarta_in_wayang_costumes.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mayor_of_Surakarta_and_Vice_Mayor_of_Surakarta_in_wayang_costumes.jpg) Joko Widodo and his deputy [F. X. Hadi Rudyatmo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F._X._Hadi_Rudyatmo "F. X. Hadi Rudyatmo") in a traditional Javanese _[wayang wong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayang_wong "Wayang wong")_ costume, 2011

After joining PDI-P in 2004, Jokowi contested the [2005 mayoral election in Surakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Surakarta_mayoral_election "2005 Surakarta mayoral election") with [F. X. Hadi Rudyatmo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F._X._Hadi_Rudyatmo "F. X. Hadi Rudyatmo") as his running mate, backed by PDI-P and the [National Awakening Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Awakening_Party "National Awakening Party").[&#8202;](#ref-back-24-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-24)[&#8202;](#ref-back-25-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-25) The pair won 36.62 per cent of the vote against the incumbent [Slamet Suryanto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slamet_Suryanto "Slamet Suryanto") and two other candidates. During the campaign, many questioned his background as a property and furniture businessman. One academic study, however, argued that his leadership style succeeded in building an interactive relationship with the public, allowing him to inspire strong trust among residents.[&#8202;](#ref-back-scribd.com-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-scribd.com) He also adopted aspects of the development model of European cities, which he had frequently visited as a businessman, as a guide for changes in Surakarta.[&#8202;](#ref-back-27-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-27)

His notable policies as mayor included[&#8202;](#ref-back-28-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-28) the construction of new traditional markets and the renovation of existing ones, the creation of a 7-kilometre city walk with a 3-metre-wide pedestrian path along Surakarta's main street, the revitalisation of Balekambang and Sriwedari parks, stricter rules on cutting down trees along major roads, and the rebranding of the city as a centre of Javanese culture and tourism under the slogan "The Spirit of Java". He also promoted Surakarta as a centre for meetings, incentives, conventions and exhibitions (MICE), launched healthcare and education insurance programmes for residents, and introduced a local [bus rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_rapid_transit "Bus rapid transit") system, _[Batik Solo Trans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batik_Solo_Trans "Batik Solo Trans")_, as well as Solo Techno Park, which helped support the [Esemka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esemka "Esemka") Indonesian car project.[&#8202;](#ref-back-29-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-29)

It was during his tenure as mayor that he began the _blusukan_ practice, making impromptu visits to neighbourhoods to hear residents' concerns directly, a style that later became a hallmark of his political career. He also barred family members from bidding for city projects, which he said was intended to reduce the risk of corruption. Some of his policies brought him into conflict with the then governor of Central Java, [Bibit Waluyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibit_Waluyo "Bibit Waluyo"), who at one point called Jokowi a "fool" over his opposition to a provincial construction project in Surakarta.[&#8202;](#ref-back-30-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-30)

His supporters pointed to rapid changes in Surakarta under his leadership and to the city's branding under the motto 'Solo: The Spirit of Java'. While in office, he relocated antique stalls in Banjarsari Gardens without unrest, a move presented as part of the effort to restore the site's role as open green space. He also stressed the importance of business participation in community life and improved communication with residents through regular appearances on local television. As part of the city's rebranding, he applied for Surakarta to join the [Organization of World Heritage Cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_World_Heritage_Cities "Organization of World Heritage Cities"), which accepted the city in 2006, and Surakarta was later chosen to host the organisation's conference in October 2008.[&#8202;](#ref-back-31-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-31)

In 2007, Surakarta also hosted the World Music Festival (Festival Musik Dunia/FMD) at the [Fort Vastenburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Vastenburg "Fort Vastenburg") complex near the city centre.[&#8202;](#ref-back-32-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-32) The following year, the festival was held in the [Mangkunegaran Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangkunegaran_Palace "Mangkunegaran Palace") complex.[&#8202;](#ref-back-33-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-33)

Part of Jokowi's style was a populist, can-do approach (_punya gaye_) intended to build ties with a broad electorate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-scribd.com-1)[\[26\]](#refnote-scribd.com) As mayor, he became personally involved in an incident just before Christmas 2011, when the Surakarta municipality had overdue electricity bills of nearly $1 million (IDR 8.9 billion) owed to the state-owned electricity company _[Perusahaan Listrik Negara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perusahaan_Listrik_Negara "Perusahaan Listrik Negara")_ (PLN).[&#8202;](#ref-back-34-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-34) As part of a stricter policy on collecting overdue bills, PLN cut power to the city's street lights just before Christmas. The city government quickly authorised payment but also protested that PLN should have considered the public interest before acting. To reinforce the point, Jokowi made a widely publicised visit to the local PLN office to deliver the IDR 8.9 billion in cash, in the form of hundreds of bundles of banknotes and even small coins.[&#8202;](#ref-back-35-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-35)

In 2010, he was [re-elected for a second term](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Surakarta_mayoral_election "2010 Surakarta mayoral election"), again with Hadi as his running mate. They won 90.09 per cent of the vote and lost in only a single polling station.[&#8202;](#ref-back-36-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-36) He was later named _Tempo_ magazine's 'Leader of Choice' in 2008 and received a 'Changemakers Award' from [Republika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republika_(Indonesian_newspaper) "Republika (Indonesian newspaper)") in 2010. His name also began to appear in national polls for the Jakarta governorship well before PDI-P formally nominated him, including surveys by the [University of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Indonesia "University of Indonesia") and Cyrus Network in 2011.[&#8202;](#ref-back-scribd.com-2)[\[26\]](#refnote-scribd.com)

In 2012, after declaring his intention to run in the [Jakarta gubernatorial election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Jakarta_gubernatorial_election "2012 Jakarta gubernatorial election"), Jokowi faced a smear campaign.[&#8202;](#ref-back-37-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-37) A group calling itself the _Save Solo, Save Jakarta and Save Indonesia Team_ (TS3) reported him to the Corruption Eradication Commission (KPK), alleging that he had facilitated the misuse of education funds by subordinates in Surakarta in 2010. The KPK investigated the allegation, found that it was based on false data, and stated that there was no indication Jokowi had misappropriated funds.[&#8202;](#ref-back-38-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-38)

### Governor of Jakarta

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5c/Gubernur_DKI_Jokowi.jpg/330px-Gubernur_DKI_Jokowi.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Gubernur_DKI_Jokowi.jpg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Gubernur_DKI_Joko_Widodo.jpg/250px-Gubernur_DKI_Joko_Widodo.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Gubernur_DKI_Joko_Widodo.jpg)

Two versions of Widodo's official portrait as [governor of Jakarta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Jakarta "Governor of Jakarta"), 2012

Despite disappointment among some Surakarta residents that he would not complete his second term as mayor,[&#8202;](#ref-back-39-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-39) Jokowi ran in the 2012 Jakarta gubernatorial election and defeated the incumbent [Fauzi Bowo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauzi_Bowo "Fauzi Bowo") in a runoff.[&#8202;](#ref-back-40-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-40) His inner circle of advisers in Jakarta reportedly included figures such as F. X. Hadi 'Rudy' Rudyatmo, Sumartono Hadinoto and Anggit Nugroho, who had worked with him while he was mayor of Surakarta, as well as [Basuki Tjahaja Purnama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basuki_Tjahaja_Purnama "Basuki Tjahaja Purnama") ("Ahok"), his deputy governor.[&#8202;](#ref-back-41-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-41)[&#8202;](#ref-back-42-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-42) Jokowi continued the _blusukan_ style he had adopted as mayor of Surakarta by regularly visiting neighbourhoods, especially slum areas. During these visits, he wore simple informal clothes and stopped at markets or walked through narrow alleys to hear residents' concerns directly, including food prices, housing problems, flooding and transport. Polling and media coverage suggested that this hands-on approach was highly popular both in Jakarta and elsewhere in Indonesia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-43-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-43)

After he took office, tax revenues and Jakarta's provincial budget rose significantly, from IDR 41 trillion in 2012 to IDR 72 trillion in 2014.[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-44) Jokowi and Ahok both publicised their monthly salaries and the provincial budget.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbcjokowi-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-bbcjokowi)[&#8202;](#ref-back-46-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-46) They also introduced programmes aimed at improving transparency, including online tax reporting, e-budgeting, e-purchasing and a cash-management system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbcjokowi-1)[\[45\]](#refnote-bbcjokowi) In addition, meetings and activities attended by Jokowi and Ahok were recorded and uploaded to YouTube.[&#8202;](#ref-back-47-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-47)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Jokowi_blusukan.jpg/960px-Jokowi_blusukan.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Jokowi_blusukan.jpg) Jokowi on a _blusukan_ neighborhood visit in Jakarta

In healthcare, Jokowi introduced the universal healthcare programme known as the 'Healthy Jakarta Card' (_Kartu Jakarta Sehat_, KJS).[&#8202;](#ref-back-48-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-48) It combined an insurance scheme administered through the state-owned insurer PT Askes Indonesia ( _Persero_) with a plan to regulate treatment charges for more than 20,000 services and procedures.[&#8202;](#ref-back-49-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-49) The programme was criticised over confusion in its implementation and long queues,[&#8202;](#ref-back-50-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-50) though Jokowi defended it and urged patience. In education, he launched the 'Smart Jakarta Card' (_Kartu Jakarta Pintar_, KJP) on 1 December 2012 to assist students from low-income families. The card provided an allowance that could be withdrawn from ATMs to pay for school needs such as books and uniforms.[&#8202;](#ref-back-51-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-51)

Other notable policies of his administration included a bureaucratic recruitment system known as _lelang jabatan_ (lit.'auction of office position'), which gave civil servants an equal chance to compete for certain posts by meeting the required qualifications;[&#8202;](#ref-back-52-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-52) efforts to regulate the chaotic concentration of street vendors in [Pasar Minggu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasar_Minggu "Pasar Minggu") and Pasar Tanah Abang;[&#8202;](#ref-back-53-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-53)[&#8202;](#ref-back-54-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-54) dredging and reservoir-normalisation projects to reduce flooding;[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-55)[&#8202;](#ref-back-56-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-56)[&#8202;](#ref-back-57-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-57) and the commencement of the long-delayed [Jakarta MRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakarta_MRT "Jakarta MRT") and [Jakarta LRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakarta_Light_Rail_Transit "Jakarta Light Rail Transit") projects.[&#8202;](#ref-back-58-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-58)[&#8202;](#ref-back-59-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-59) As governor, Jokowi also appointed a non-Muslim _lurah_ (subdistrict chief) to the Muslim-majority subdistrict of Lenteng Agung despite protests from some residents.[&#8202;](#ref-back-60-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-60) Former deputy governor [Prijanto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prijanto?action=edit&redlink=1 "Prijanto") claimed that Jokowi had committed maladministration by formalising another expired certificate in relation to the BMW Park government asset certificate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-61-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-61)

In 2013, Jokowi was reported to the [National Commission on Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Commission_on_Human_Rights_(Indonesia) "National Commission on Human Rights (Indonesia)") over the eviction of squatters near [Pluit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluit "Pluit"). In earlier "political contracts", he had promised not to relocate residents to distant places.[&#8202;](#ref-back-62-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-62)[&#8202;](#ref-back-63-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-63) Jokowi met with Pluit residents and Komnas HAM to explain that the evictions were necessary to restore water catchment areas in order to reduce flooding, and that affected families were being moved to low-cost apartments.[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-64)[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-65)

## Elections

Jokowi's popularity in Indonesian politics and the economy has been termed the "Jokowi Effect". When he was declared a candidate in the [2014 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Indonesian_presidential_election "2014 Indonesian presidential election"), it was believed to have boosted the popularity of the [Indonesian Democratic Party of Struggle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_Democratic_Party_of_Struggle "Indonesian Democratic Party of Struggle") to 30 per cent in the [2014 legislative election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Indonesian_legislative_election "2014 Indonesian legislative election").[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-66) In the capital market, the effect was also said to have boosted the Indonesian stock market and the [Rupiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupiah "Rupiah"), as Jokowi was regarded as having a clean track record.[&#8202;](#ref-back-67-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-67) The Jokowi Effect was likewise widely cited as a factor in [Prabowo Subianto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prabowo_Subianto "Prabowo Subianto")'s victory in the [2024 Indonesian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Indonesian_presidential_election "2024 Indonesian presidential election").[&#8202;](#ref-back-68-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-68)[&#8202;](#ref-back-69-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-69)

### 2014 general election

See also: [2014 Indonesian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Indonesian_presidential_election "2014 Indonesian presidential election") and [Joko Widodo 2014 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joko_Widodo_2014_presidential_campaign "Joko Widodo 2014 presidential campaign")

[Megawati Sukarnoputri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megawati_Sukarnoputri "Megawati Sukarnoputri") nominated Jokowi as her party's presidential candidate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-70) During the campaign, a social-media volunteer team, _JASMEV_, made a provocative statement threatening that Islam would not be given space in Indonesia if Jokowi won the 2014 election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-71)[&#8202;](#ref-back-72-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-72) The group was paid IDR 500 million to campaign for the Joko Widodo–Jusuf Kalla ticket during the election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-73)

After quick-count results from multiple polling agencies were released, Jokowi declared victory on 9 July. His opponent [Prabowo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prabowo_Subianto "Prabowo Subianto") also claimed victory, creating confusion among the public.[&#8202;](#ref-back-74-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-74) On 22 July, hours before the formal announcement of the result, Prabowo withdrew.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Tempo1-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-Tempo1) Jokowi's victory was confirmed later that day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-76-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-76)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Tempo1-1)[\[75\]](#refnote-Tempo1) The [General Elections Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Elections_Commission "General Elections Commission") (KPU) gave him a narrow victory with 53.15 per cent of the vote (almost 71 million votes), against Prabowo's 46.85 per cent (62 million votes),[&#8202;](#ref-back-77-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-77) though Prabowo's camp disputed the tally.[&#8202;](#ref-back-78-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-78)

After his victory, Jokowi said that, having grown up under the authoritarian and corrupt [New Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Order_(Indonesia) "New Order (Indonesia)") regime, he would never have expected someone from a lower-class background to become president. _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times")_ quoted him as saying, "Now, it's quite similar to America, yeah? There is the [American dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_dream "American dream"), and here we have the Indonesian dream."[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-NYT) Jokowi was the first Indonesian president from outside the military and political elite, and political commentator Salim Said described the popular view of him as "someone who is our neighbour, who decided to get into politics and run for president."[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT-1)[\[79\]](#refnote-NYT)

### 2019 general election

See also: [2019 Indonesian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Indonesian_general_election "2019 Indonesian general election") and [Joko Widodo 2019 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joko_Widodo_2019_presidential_campaign "Joko Widodo 2019 presidential campaign")

In 2018, Jokowi announced that he would run for re-election [the following year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Indonesian_general_election "2019 Indonesian general election"). His vice president [Jusuf Kalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jusuf_Kalla "Jusuf Kalla") was ineligible for another term because of the term limits on the offices of president and vice president, having already served a five-year term as vice president during [Susilo Bambang Yudhoyono](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susilo_Bambang_Yudhoyono "Susilo Bambang Yudhoyono")'s first administration (2004–2009). Speculation over Jokowi's choice of running mate centred on several figures, including [Mahfud MD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahfud_MD "Mahfud MD"), a former defence minister and chief justice of the [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Indonesia "Constitutional Court of Indonesia"). In a surprise move, Jokowi announced that [Ma'ruf Amin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'ruf_Amin "Ma'ruf Amin") would be his running mate. Mahfud had reportedly been preparing for the vice-presidential candidacy. Ma'ruf was selected instead following pressure from several constituent parties in Jokowi's governing coalition and from influential Islamic figures.[&#8202;](#ref-back-80-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-80) Explaining his decision, Jokowi cited Ma'ruf's long experience in government and religious affairs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-81)

The KPU officially announced in the early hours of 21 May 2019 that the Joko Widodo–Ma'ruf Amin ticket had won the election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-82) The official tally gave Jokowi 85 million votes (55.50 per cent) and Prabowo 68 million votes (44.50 per cent).[&#8202;](#ref-back-83-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-83) Prabowo supporters protested the result in Jakarta, and the unrest escalated into [riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_2019_Jakarta_protests_and_riots "May 2019 Jakarta protests and riots") that left eight people dead and more than 600 injured.[&#8202;](#ref-back-8d-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-8d) Prabowo's campaign team later brought a case to the Constitutional Court, which rejected the challenge in its entirety.[&#8202;](#ref-back-85-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-85)

## Presidency (2014–2024)

Main article: [Post-Suharto era in Indonesia §Presidency of Widodo (2014–2024)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Suharto_era_in_Indonesia#Presidency_of_Widodo_(2014%E2%80%932024) "Post-Suharto era in Indonesia")

See also: [First inauguration of Joko Widodo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Joko_Widodo "First inauguration of Joko Widodo") and [Second inauguration of Joko Widodo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_inauguration_of_Joko_Widodo "Second inauguration of Joko Widodo")

### Cabinets

See also: [Working Cabinet (2014–2019)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_Cabinet_(2014%E2%80%932019) "Working Cabinet (2014–2019)") and [Onward Indonesia Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onward_Indonesia_Cabinet "Onward Indonesia Cabinet")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Kabinet_Kerja_Jokowi-JK_2014.jpg/500px-Kabinet_Kerja_Jokowi-JK_2014.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kabinet_Kerja_Jokowi-JK_2014.jpg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0f/Kabinet_Indonesia_Maju_1.jpg/500px-Kabinet_Indonesia_Maju_1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kabinet_Indonesia_Maju_1.jpg)

Joko Widodo's initial cabinet lines-up in 2014 (top) and 2019 (bottom)

Despite vowing during the 2014 campaign not to distribute government posts simply to political allies, Jokowi's first cabinet included many members of political parties.[&#8202;](#ref-back-86-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-86)[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-87) During the first year of his administration, he led a [minority government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minority_government "Minority government") until [Golkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golkar "Golkar"), the second-largest party in the [People's Representative Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Representative_Council "People's Representative Council") (DPR), switched from the opposition to the governing coalition. Jokowi denied accusations that he had interfered in Golkar's internal affairs, although he acknowledged that Luhut might have influenced the shift.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ReformWinds-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-ReformWinds) His [minister of industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Industry_(Indonesia) "Ministry of Industry (Indonesia)"), [Airlangga Hartarto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airlangga_Hartarto "Airlangga Hartarto"), was elected chairman of Golkar in 2018.[&#8202;](#ref-back-89-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-89) The [National Mandate Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Mandate_Party "National Mandate Party") (PAN) had also moved into the government camp earlier, but returned to the opposition in 2018.[&#8202;](#ref-back-90-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-90)[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-91)

Jokowi announced the 34 members of his first cabinet on 26 October 2014.[&#8202;](#ref-back-92-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-92) It was praised for the inclusion of women, with [Retno Marsudi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retno_Marsudi "Retno Marsudi") becoming Indonesia's first female foreign minister, but also drew criticism for several appointments seen as politically motivated, including that of [Puan Maharani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puan_Maharani "Puan Maharani"), daughter of [Megawati Sukarnoputri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megawati_Sukarnoputri "Megawati Sukarnoputri").[&#8202;](#ref-back-93-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-93) His administration also created two new ministries—the Ministry of Public Works and Housing and the [Ministry of Environment and Forestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Environment_and_Forestry_(Indonesia) "Ministry of Environment and Forestry (Indonesia)")—through mergers of existing ministries, while renaming and reorganising others.[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-94) Jokowi carried out three cabinet reshuffles by 2018, removing ministers such as [Rizal Ramli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rizal_Ramli "Rizal Ramli") and [Bambang Brodjonegoro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bambang_Brodjonegoro "Bambang Brodjonegoro") while bringing in figures including Luhut and former [World Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Bank "World Bank") managing director [Sri Mulyani Indrawati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Mulyani_Indrawati "Sri Mulyani Indrawati").[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-95) A further reshuffle followed in December 2020, replacing six ministers, including two who had been apprehended by the KPK.[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-96)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5f/Jokowi_selfie_with_Prabowo_and_reporters.jpg/500px-Jokowi_selfie_with_Prabowo_and_reporters.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Jokowi_selfie_with_Prabowo_and_reporters.jpg) Joko Widodo and [Prabowo Subianto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prabowo_Subianto "Prabowo Subianto") in October 2019

Jokowi was criticised by PDI-P over what it saw as weaknesses in his policies, and PDI-P legislator [Effendi Simbolon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effendi_Simbolon "Effendi Simbolon") called for his impeachment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-97) On 9 April 2015, during a PDI-P congress, party leader [Megawati Sukarnoputri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Megawati_Sukarnoputri "Megawati Sukarnoputri") referred to Jokowi as a party functionary. She noted that presidential candidates are nominated by political parties, implying that Jokowi owed his position to the party and should follow its policy line.[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-98)[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-99) Several months earlier, Megawati and Jokowi had disagreed over the appointment of a new police chief, with Megawati backing Budi Gunawan, her former adjutant, while Jokowi preferred [Badrodin Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badrodin_Haiti "Badrodin Haiti").[&#8202;](#ref-back-ReformWinds-1)[\[88\]](#refnote-ReformWinds)[&#8202;](#ref-back-100-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-100)[&#8202;](#ref-back-101-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-101)

After his re-election, Jokowi announced his second cabinet on 23 October 2019. He retained several ministers, including Sri Mulyani and Luhut, while also appointing [Gojek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gojek "Gojek") founder [Nadiem Makarim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nadiem_Makarim "Nadiem Makarim") and his two-time presidential rival [Prabowo Subianto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prabowo_Subianto "Prabowo Subianto") as education and defence ministers, respectively.[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-102)

In the first year of his second term, Jokowi's approval rating fell to 45.2 per cent, while his disapproval rating rose to 52 per cent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-103-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-103)[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-104) His deputy, Ma'ruf Amin, had a 67 per cent disapproval rating. The decline was attributed to unpopular policies. By early 2023, however, Jokowi's approval rating had reached an all-time high of 76.2 per cent after the easing of COVID-19 restrictions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-105-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-105)

### Politics

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Joko_Widodo_Palu_earthquake_Petobo_residential.jpg/500px-Joko_Widodo_Palu_earthquake_Petobo_residential.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Joko_Widodo_Palu_earthquake_Petobo_residential.jpg) Jokowi visiting the destroyed village of [Petobo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petobo "Petobo") after the [2018 Sulawesi earthquake and tsunami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Sulawesi_earthquake_and_tsunami "2018 Sulawesi earthquake and tsunami")

Early in his first term, the opposition coalition in the DPR attempted to revoke a regulation ( _Perppu_, Government Regulation in Lieu of Acts) issued by Jokowi's predecessor that had guaranteed direct regional elections in Indonesia, overriding an earlier law that provided for indirect elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-106-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-106) Jokowi supported direct regional elections and opposed efforts to revoke the regulation, stating that "direct regional elections was, in principle, non-negotiable".[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-107) Within the first three years of his administration, he issued four such _Perppu_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-108) Jokowi's government, which included parties that had opposed him in presidential elections, has been described as a [big tent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_tent "Big tent") government and, by former Singaporean foreign minister [George Yeo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Yeo "George Yeo"), as "democracy with Javanese characteristics".[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-109)

After his re-election in 2019, a number of prominent politicians began floating the idea of amending the constitution to allow Jokowi to seek a third term in 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-110-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-110)[&#8202;](#ref-back-111-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-111) Cabinet figures who voiced support for extending term limits included coordinating ministers [Airlangga Hartarto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airlangga_Hartarto "Airlangga Hartarto") and [Luhut Binsar Pandjaitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luhut_Binsar_Pandjaitan "Luhut Binsar Pandjaitan").[&#8202;](#ref-back-112-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-112) The proposal gained greater prominence after the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic "COVID-19 pandemic"), amid claims that government programmes in his second term had been disrupted. By April 2022, however, Jokowi had explicitly stated that he would not seek a third term and ordered his cabinet to stop publicly discussing either a term-limit extension or an election delay.[&#8202;](#ref-back-113-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-113)

Although he was not running for a third term, Jokowi told media outlets that he would not be a neutral actor[&#8202;](#ref-back-114-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-114) and that he would "meddle" in the [2024 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Indonesian_general_election "2024 Indonesian general election"). He also met political party leaders in his capacity as president.[&#8202;](#ref-back-115-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-115)[&#8202;](#ref-back-116-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-116)[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-117) In September 2023, during a public rally, he said that he possessed intelligence information gathered by government agencies, including [BIN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Intelligence_Agency_(Indonesia) "State Intelligence Agency (Indonesia)") and [BAIS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_Strategic_Intelligence_Agency "Indonesian Strategic Intelligence Agency"), on the internal workings of political parties.[&#8202;](#ref-back-118-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-118)[&#8202;](#ref-back-119-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-119) In October 2023, Constitutional Court chief justice [Anwar Usman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anwar_Usman "Anwar Usman"), Jokowi's brother-in-law, issued a ruling that created a loophole in the minimum age requirement for presidential and vice-presidential candidates, allowing Jokowi's son [Gibran Rakabuming Raka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gibran_Rakabuming_Raka "Gibran Rakabuming Raka") to run as Prabowo's running mate in 2024. Usman was later reprimanded by a judicial panel over the ruling and removed as chief justice.[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-120) On 12 February 2024, investigative journalist [Dandhy Laksono](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dandhy_Laksono "Dandhy Laksono") released _Dirty Vote_, a documentary on YouTube alleging that Jokowi had used state funds to support Prabowo's campaign.[&#8202;](#ref-back-121-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-121) Prabowo went on to win the election by a wide margin, which a number of analysts attributed to Jokowi's backing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-122-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-122)[&#8202;](#ref-back-123-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-123)[&#8202;](#ref-back-124-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-124) Jokowi was later also accused of interfering in the [2024 Indonesian local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Indonesian_local_elections "2024 Indonesian local elections") because of controversies over candidate-age requirements, notably affecting his son [Kaesang Pangarep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaesang_Pangarep "Kaesang Pangarep"), and over political support, notably affecting [Anies Baswedan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anies_Baswedan "Anies Baswedan"), a prominent critic of his administration.[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-125)

Near the end of his second term, however, Jokowi's relationship with PDI-P deteriorated because he backed [Prabowo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prabowo_Subianto "Prabowo Subianto") in the [2024 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prabowo_Subianto_2024_presidential_campaign "Prabowo Subianto 2024 presidential campaign") rather than his own party's candidate, [Ganjar Pranowo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganjar_Pranowo "Ganjar Pranowo"); Jokowi's eldest son, [Gibran Rakabuming Raka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gibran_Rakabuming_Raka "Gibran Rakabuming Raka"), also ran as Prabowo's vice-presidential candidate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-126-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-126) On 22 April 2024, after the Constitutional Court rejected all claims and disputes relating to the 2024 presidential election, the PDI-P Honorary Council declared that Jokowi and Gibran were no longer members of the party,[&#8202;](#ref-back-127-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-127)[&#8202;](#ref-back-128-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-128) confirming their break with PDI-P. Even so, they were allowed to retain their membership cards as a gesture of respect for Jokowi as the sitting president and Gibran as the incoming vice president, although the cards no longer carried any rights within the party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-129-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-129) The expulsion was declared complete on 4 December 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-130-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-130)[&#8202;](#ref-back-131-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-131)

### Law and human rights

See also: [Bali Nine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bali_Nine "Bali Nine") and [Capital punishment in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_Indonesia "Capital punishment in Indonesia")

Judicial executions in Indonesia are carried out under a presidential decree following a death sentence imposed by a trial court.[&#8202;](#ref-back-132-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-132) Jokowi in 2015 said he would not grant [clemency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clemency "Clemency") for drug offenders sentenced to death, arguing Indonesia was in a [state of emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_emergency "State of emergency") over [drug-related crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drug-related_crime "Drug-related crime"), citing statistics the _[Jakarta Globe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakarta_Globe "Jakarta Globe")_ reported to be faulty.[&#8202;](#ref-back-133-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-133)[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-134) His stance drew criticism as it could harm relations with the native countries of the condemned convicts,[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-135) and also imperil Indonesians facing the death penalty abroad.[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-136)[&#8202;](#ref-back-137-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-137) Australia, Brazil and the Netherlands recalled their ambassadors from Indonesia following multiple executions in 2015.[&#8202;](#ref-back-balinine-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-balinine)[&#8202;](#ref-back-139-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-139) Australia reduced its foreign aid to Indonesia by nearly half,[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-140) and [Amnesty International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amnesty_International "Amnesty International") issued a condemnation saying they showed a "complete disregard for due process and human rights safeguards".[&#8202;](#ref-back-141-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-141) Former [Indonesian Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Indonesia "Constitutional Court of Indonesia") chief justice [Jimly Asshiddiqie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimly_Asshiddiqie "Jimly Asshiddiqie"), who was a key player in the anti-death penalty lobby in Jakarta, said the push for the execution of Australians [Myuran Sukumaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myuran_Sukumaran "Myuran Sukumaran") and [Andrew Chan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Chan_(drug_smuggler) "Andrew Chan (drug smuggler)") had come from Jokowi personally.[&#8202;](#ref-back-142-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-142) _[The Sydney Morning Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sydney_Morning_Herald "The Sydney Morning Herald")_ reported that Jokowi did not have or read related documents when he refused their clemency requests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-documents-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-documents) In the same year, Jokowi granted Frenchman Serge Atlaoui and [Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipinos "Filipinos") [Mary Jane Veloso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Jane_Veloso "Mary Jane Veloso") temporary reprieves due to pending legal appeals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-documents-1)[\[143\]](#refnote-documents) As of 2017, around 260 people remain on death row in Indonesia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-144-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-144)

Regarding terrorism, Jokowi's administration in early 2016 proposed replacing the 2003 anti-terrorism law. Following the 2018 [Surabaya bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surabaya_bombings "Surabaya bombings"), the worst terrorist attack on Indonesian soil since the [2002 Bali bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Bali_bombings "2002 Bali bombings"), the controversial bill passed, allowing the [Indonesian National Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_National_Armed_Forces "Indonesian National Armed Forces") to participate in [counter-terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-terrorism "Counter-terrorism") activities upon police request and presidential approval.[&#8202;](#ref-back-145-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-145) It also allowed extended detention of terror suspects and permitted wiretapping without initial court approval.[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-146) Jokowi had threatened to issue a presidential regulation in lieu of law ( _Perppu_) if the bill did not pass the parliament by June that year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-147-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-147)

During Jokowi's administration, there have been numerous instances where people were arrested or reported to police for activities deemed insulting to the president.[&#8202;](#ref-back-148-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-148)[&#8202;](#ref-back-149-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-149) Rights activists deem such arrests as a violation of the [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Indonesia "Constitution of Indonesia")'s guarantee of freedom of speech.[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-150)[&#8202;](#ref-back-151-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-151) A group claiming to be Jokowi's supporters reported [_Tempo_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tempo_(Indonesian_magazine) "Tempo (Indonesian magazine)") magazine to police over a caricature of Jokowi as [Pinocchio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinocchio "Pinocchio"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-152)[&#8202;](#ref-back-153-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-153) after which the Presidential Palace issued a statement saying "the President respected freedom of press and speech".[&#8202;](#ref-back-154-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-154) A book about Jokowi titled _Jokowi Undercover_ was banned upon release and its author sentenced to three years in prison[&#8202;](#ref-back-155-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-155) and buyers of the book being advised to surrender their copies to the authorities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-156-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-156) _Tempo_ magazine described the 436-page book as "trashy and tasteless, a compilation of hoax reports on President Joko Widodo, scattered across the internet and cyber chatrooms".[&#8202;](#ref-back-157-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-157) The government's plans to resurrect a Dutch colonial law that would permit imprisonment for insulting the president [resulted in widespread protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Indonesian_protests_and_riots "2019 Indonesian protests and riots").[&#8202;](#ref-back-158-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-158)[&#8202;](#ref-back-159-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-159) A Law Firm and Public Interest Law Office (AMAR) institution later reported following the protests that they received many complaints of students regarding threats and sanctions of expulsion or suspension from their schools and universities.[&#8202;](#ref-back-160-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-160) In addition, a remission granted to a journalist's murderer was revoked following media criticism.[&#8202;](#ref-back-161-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-161)[&#8202;](#ref-back-162-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-162)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/8d/Tolak_Omnibus_Law.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tolak_Omnibus_Law.jpg) Since 2019, [a series of mass protests and civil unrests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesia_omnibus_law_protests#See_also "Indonesia omnibus law protests") were held across the country against some controversial policies.

In response to major protests, Jokowi's administration has generated some controversies. On 22 May 2019, amid post-election riots by supporters of losing presidential candidate Prabowo Subianto, the government limited the speed at which photos and videos could be shared on social media to stop people from being incited by fake news and calls for violence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-163-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-163)[&#8202;](#ref-back-164-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-164) In the aftermath, [Amnesty International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amnesty_International "Amnesty International")'s Indonesian office denounced repressive measures against the demonstrators, condemned them as a grave human rights abuse and demanded the government investigate the extrajudicial executions in the clashes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-165-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-165)[&#8202;](#ref-back-166-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-166) In August and September 2019, the government blocked internet access in Papua and West Papua provinces amid [violent protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Papua_protests "2019 Papua protests") against racism. Jakarta State Administrative Court in 2020 ruled the internet blocks in Papua illegal.[&#8202;](#ref-back-167-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-167)

In 2017, Jokowi supported a controversial bill on mass organisations, which upon passing resulted in the disbandment of the Indonesian branch of [Hizb ut-Tahrir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hizb_ut-Tahrir "Hizb ut-Tahrir"). He argued the law was necessary to defend the national ideology, [Pancasila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pancasila_(politics) "Pancasila (politics)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-168-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-168) The 2020 banning of the [Islamic Defenders Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Defenders_Front "Islamic Defenders Front") (FPI) was also based on that law.[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-169) Twenty-three days' earlier, police had shot dead six FPI members during a confrontation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-6supporter-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-6supporter)[&#8202;](#ref-back-171-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-171) The president's subsequent defence of the police during their duty and his statement that no citizens should break the law or harm the country[&#8202;](#ref-back-172-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-172)[&#8202;](#ref-back-173-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-173) was criticised by FPI secretary-general Munarman as a justification of human rights abuse and [structural violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structural_violence "Structural violence").[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-174) A police chief involved in the car chase and subsequent murder claimed that the members were armed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-6supporter-1)[\[170\]](#refnote-6supporter) After the passing of several controversial bills and repressive crackdowns from security officers on major protests since 2019,[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-175) his presidency has been criticised for "neo-authoritarianism".[&#8202;](#ref-back-176-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-176)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Demokrasi.co.id-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-Demokrasi.co.id)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CNN_Indonesia-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-CNN_Indonesia)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Banyan-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-Banyan) The _[South China Morning Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_China_Morning_Post "South China Morning Post")_ even named him 'Little [Suharto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suharto "Suharto").'[&#8202;](#ref-back-little-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-little)

A premium price hike of public health care [BPJS Kesehatan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BPJS_Kesehatan "BPJS Kesehatan") through Executive Order ( _Perpres_) 64/2020 was criticised as a flagrant breach of permanent [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Indonesia "Supreme Court of Indonesia") ( _Mahkamah Agung_) decision[&#8202;](#ref-back-181-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-181) that nullified the _Perpres_ 82/2018 about the price hike. The _Perpres_ 64/2020 itself was signed amid the COVID-19 pandemic that had caused hardship among the population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-182-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-182)[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-183) His former deputy mayor of Surakarta, [F. X. Hadi Rudyatmo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F._X._Hadi_Rudyatmo "F. X. Hadi Rudyatmo"), also voiced similar concerns.[&#8202;](#ref-back-184-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-184)

Jokowi's presidency coincided with the 50th anniversary of the [Indonesian mass killings of 1965–66](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_mass_killings_of_1965%E2%80%9366 "Indonesian mass killings of 1965–66") in 2015. A government-supported symposium to resolve human rights violations surrounding the event was held in 2016, but Jokowi said his government would not apologise to the victims of the mass purge.[&#8202;](#ref-back-185-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-185)[&#8202;](#ref-back-186-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-186) On [LGBT rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights "LGBT rights"), Jokowi stated that "there should be no discrimination against anyone", but added that "in terms of our beliefs, \[&#8202;](#ref-back-187-0)[the LGBT lifestyle\] isn't allowed, Islam does not allow it."[\[187\]](#refnote-187) Under his presidency, the controversial [transmigration program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transmigration_program "Transmigration program") was cut once more, when in 2015, it was decided to end the migration program to the Papuan provinces.[&#8202;](#ref-back-188-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-188)

Following the July 2022 [murder of Nofriansyah Yosua Hutabarat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murder_of_Nofriansyah_Yosua_Hutabarat "Murder of Nofriansyah Yosua Hutabarat"), an Indonesian police officer, there were allegations of police involvement in a cover-up. Police chief General [Listyo Sigit Prabowo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Listyo_Sigit_Prabowo "Listyo Sigit Prabowo") took over the investigation, forming a special team including members of the Human Rights Commission and Police Commissions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sentinel-08Aug2022-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-Sentinel-08Aug2022) Jokowi was keen for the force to be open about what happens: "Open it as it is. No cover-up. Transparent. That's it. This is important so that the people don't have doubts over the incident that occurred. This is what has to be maintained. Public trust in the police must be maintained."[&#8202;](#ref-back-Barrett-26Jul2022-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-Barrett-26Jul2022) In August 2022, Hutabarat's former superior, Inspector General Ferdy Sambo, head of internal affairs of the Indonesian National Police, along with three others, was charged with Hutabarat's murder.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Barrett-10Aug2022-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-Barrett-10Aug2022)[&#8202;](#ref-back-McBeth-11Aug2022-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-McBeth-11Aug2022)

On 27 December 2023, hundreds of students from various universities in [Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aceh "Aceh"), such as Abulyatama University, Bina Bangsa Getsempena University, and [University of Muhammadiyah Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Muhammadiyah_Aceh "University of Muhammadiyah Aceh"), stormed a shelter for [Rohingya refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya_people "Rohingya people") and forced them out of a convention centre in the city of [Banda Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banda_Aceh "Banda Aceh"), demanding them to be deported.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-:3)[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-194) The students were also seen kicking the belongings of the Rohingya men, women, and children who were seated on the floor and crying in fear. They burned tires and chanted "Kick them out" and "Reject Rohingya in Aceh." Widodo later responded by inviting society to maintain tolerance and peace and to unite in maintaining a sense of unity, cooperation, and humanity, as well as being alert to world crises, such as food and economic crises, disputes between nations, and war.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-1)[\[193\]](#refnote-:3)[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-195)

On 31 January 2024, a group of faculty members at [Gadjah Mada University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gadjah_Mada_University "Gadjah Mada University"), which Widodo attended, issued the Bulaksumur petition, criticizing his presidency and calling on him to uphold democracy and social justice.[&#8202;](#ref-back-196-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-196)[&#8202;](#ref-back-197-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-197)

### Economy

Before taking office, Widodo sought to have outgoing president [Susilo Bambang Yudhoyono](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susilo_Bambang_Yudhoyono "Susilo Bambang Yudhoyono") (SBY) take responsibility for a further increase in fuel prices[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc.com-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-bbc.com) through further cuts to subsidies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-199-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-199) Previous attempts by SBY to do so had led to civil unrest.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc.com-1)[\[198\]](#refnote-bbc.com) On 1 January 2015, Jokowi introduced measures that appeared to reduce fuel subsidies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-200-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-200)[&#8202;](#ref-back-201-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-201) The policy prompted demonstrations, which Jokowi said were necessary to redirect spending toward infrastructure, education and health.[&#8202;](#ref-back-202-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-202) From March 2015, however, the government set the price of Premium-branded petrol below market rates, effectively shifting the subsidy burden to the state-owned oil company [Pertamina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pertamina "Pertamina") rather than the central government budget.[&#8202;](#ref-back-203-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-203) The government also introduced a single-price policy aimed at selling fuel through official channels at the same price across the country, including in remote parts of [Kalimantan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalimantan "Kalimantan") and [Papua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_Papua "Indonesian Papua"). It said this target had been achieved by 2017.[&#8202;](#ref-back-204-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-204)

In the first quarter of 2015, year-on-year GDP growth was 4.92 per cent, and in the second quarter it fell to 4.6 per cent, the lowest figure since 2009.[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-205)[&#8202;](#ref-back-206-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-206)[&#8202;](#ref-back-207-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-207) Growth later remained above 5 per cent, though still below levels some observers regarded as desirable for the economy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-208-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-208) The [Indonesian rupiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_rupiah "Indonesian rupiah") (IDR) also weakened during Jokowi's administration: its exchange rate against the [U.S. dollar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_dollar "United States dollar") briefly passed IDR 15,000 in 2018, its lowest level since the [1997 Asian financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Asian_financial_crisis "1997 Asian financial crisis"), and fell further to 16,700 in 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-209-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-209)[&#8202;](#ref-back-210-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-210) Year-on-year inflation in June 2015 was 7.26 per cent, higher than in May (7.15 per cent) and in June the previous year (6.7 per cent).[&#8202;](#ref-back-211-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-211)

In 2016, the administration signed a [tax amnesty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_amnesty "Tax amnesty") law after a lengthy public debate and pushback, allowing wealthy Indonesians to declare previously unreported assets before the government tightened rules and oversight over imports and exports. It became the most successful programme of its kind in history, with more than IDR 4,865 trillion (approximately US$366 billion) in previously undeclared assets reported to the tax office.[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-212)[&#8202;](#ref-back-213-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-213)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/Joko_Widodo_at_the_opening_ceremony_of_COP26_%2810%29.jpg/500px-Joko_Widodo_at_the_opening_ceremony_of_COP26_%2810%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Joko_Widodo_at_the_opening_ceremony_of_COP26_(10).jpg) Widodo speaking at the opening ceremony of the [COP26 climate summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Nations_Climate_Change_Conference "2021 United Nations Climate Change Conference") in [Glasgow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasgow "Glasgow"), Scotland on 1 November 2021

Infrastructure development was a major feature of the Jokowi administration, with an emphasis on roads, railways, seaports, airports and irrigation. In 2016, the state budget allocated Rp 290 trillion (US$22 billion) to infrastructure, the largest such allocation in Indonesian history at the time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-214-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-214) In total, his administration planned 265 infrastructure projects beginning in 2016.[&#8202;](#ref-back-215-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-215) In September 2015, Indonesia awarded a $5.5 billion [high-speed rail project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-speed_rail_in_Indonesia "High-speed rail in Indonesia") to China,[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-216)[&#8202;](#ref-back-217-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-217) disappointing Japan, which had also sought the contract.[&#8202;](#ref-back-218-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-218) Indonesia's transport ministry later listed numerous shortcomings in the project's plans, casting doubt on its viability and highlighting the administration's difficulties in turning mega-projects into reality while seeking foreign investment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-219-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-219) Other major projects included the completion of the 4,325-kilometre [Trans Papua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans_Papua "Trans Papua") road and the [Trans-Java Toll Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Java_Toll_Road "Trans-Java Toll Road"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-220-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-220)[&#8202;](#ref-back-221-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-221) the start of construction on the [Trans-Sulawesi Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Sulawesi_Railway "Trans-Sulawesi Railway"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-222-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-222) and the [Trans-Sumatra Toll Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Sumatra_Toll_Road "Trans-Sumatra Toll Road"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-223-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-223) a US$50 billion plan to develop the maritime sector, including 24 "strategic ports",[&#8202;](#ref-back-224-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-224) and the expansion of airport capacity in remote areas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-225-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-225) The port development and modernisation programme, known as the [Sea Toll Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_Toll_Road "Sea Toll Road"), was intended to reduce price disparities between the more developed western regions and the less populated eastern regions of the country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-226-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-226)

The opposition criticised the scale of infrastructure spending, noting that Indonesia's national debt rose by 48 per cent between 2014 and March 2018 to US$181 billion. They also argued that much of the debt had been used for remuneration rather than infrastructure development.[&#8202;](#ref-back-227-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-227)[&#8202;](#ref-back-228-0)[\[228\]](#refnote-228)[&#8202;](#ref-back-229-0)[\[229\]](#refnote-229) In April 2018, Jokowi also introduced a policy allowing foreign workers in Indonesia to work without an Indonesian-language requirement,[&#8202;](#ref-back-230-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-230) arguing that this would increase investment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-231-0)[\[231\]](#refnote-231) The measure faced significant opposition from labour unions, which argued that it would raise unemployment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-232-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-232)[&#8202;](#ref-back-233-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-233)

During the [2019 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Indonesian_general_election "2019 Indonesian general election") campaign, Jokowi introduced the [Pre-Employment Card](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Employment_Card?action=edit&redlink=1 "Pre-Employment Card")\[ [id](https://id.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartu%20Prakerja "id:Kartu Prakerja")\], a training-assistance programme intended to improve workforce skills through training, retraining and upskilling. It was introduced alongside the [Smart Indonesia College Card](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smart_Indonesia_College_Card?action=edit&redlink=1 "Smart Indonesia College Card")\[ [id](https://id.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartu%20Indonesia%20Pintar%20Kuliah "id:Kartu Indonesia Pintar Kuliah")\] and the [Cheap Food Card](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheap_Food_Card?action=edit&redlink=1 "Cheap Food Card")\[ [id](https://id.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kartu%20sembako%20murah "id:Kartu sembako murah")\].[&#8202;](#ref-back-234-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-234)

Eligible recipients of the Pre-Employment Card received training funds of IDR 1,000,000, which could be used on partner digital platforms. More than 150 training institutions provided courses that could be purchased and followed online from anywhere.[&#8202;](#ref-back-235-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-235)

During the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic "COVID-19 pandemic"), the government shifted the Pre-Employment Card into a semi-social assistance programme to help workers affected by layoffs and recent graduates entering the labour market.[&#8202;](#ref-back-236-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-236) To expand its reach, the budget was doubled to IDR 20 trillion. When the programme was launched in April 2020, the government said it was intended to reduce the cost of training for workers and companies, lower the cost of accessing information about training, improve the skills and competitiveness of Indonesian workers, and supplement formal education.[&#8202;](#ref-back-237-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-237) The programme, however, remained limited to online training.

In 2020, the DPR passed the [Omnibus Law on Job Creation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omnibus_Law_on_Job_Creation "Omnibus Law on Job Creation"). Although it was intended to boost investment and reduce [red tape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_tape "Red tape"), it was also widely seen as weakening labour and environmental protections, prompting a [series of protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesia_omnibus_law_protests "Indonesia omnibus law protests") in major cities. Jokowi defended the law as necessary for job creation and urged protesters to challenge it through the [Constitutional Court of Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Indonesia "Constitutional Court of Indonesia").[&#8202;](#ref-back-238-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-238) The law, which revised more than 70 existing laws and contained around 1,200 clauses, had been proposed by Jokowi after his 2019 re-election. Several groups criticised the government's lack of transparency during its deliberation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-239-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-239) In the same year, Indonesia recorded its lowest inflation rate on record[&#8202;](#ref-back-240-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-240) and experienced its first recession since the 1997 Asian financial crisis.[&#8202;](#ref-back-241-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-241)

In November 2021, Jokowi promised at the [COP26 climate summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_United_Nations_Climate_Change_Conference "2021 United Nations Climate Change Conference") to end and reverse [deforestation in Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation_in_Indonesia "Deforestation in Indonesia") by 2030 as part of the summit's first major agreement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-242-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-242)[&#8202;](#ref-back-243-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-243) The [European Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Commission "European Commission") later approved a measure to phase out [palm oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palm_oil "Palm oil")-based [biofuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biofuel "Biofuel") by 2030.[&#8202;](#ref-back-244-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-244)[&#8202;](#ref-back-245-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-245) During a meeting with European Commission President [Ursula von der Leyen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ursula_von_der_Leyen "Ursula von der Leyen"), Jokowi expressed concern about the [EU Regulation on Deforestation-free products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU_Regulation_on_Deforestation-free_products "EU Regulation on Deforestation-free products") (EUDR), which aims to prevent products linked to [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation "Deforestation") from entering the EU market.[&#8202;](#ref-back-246-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-246)

The Jokowi administration continued its predecessor's policy of [resource nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resource_nationalism "Resource nationalism"), increasing government shareholding in multinational companies such as [Freeport McMoRan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freeport_McMoRan "Freeport McMoRan"), [TotalEnergies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TotalEnergies "TotalEnergies") and [Chevron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevron_Corporation "Chevron Corporation"). In 2018, in a move intended to reduce imports, oil companies operating in Indonesia were ordered to sell their crude oil to state-owned [Pertamina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pertamina "Pertamina").[&#8202;](#ref-back-247-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-247) A ban was also imposed on exports of raw nickel ore to encourage the growth of domestic nickel-related industries such as smelters and battery factories.[&#8202;](#ref-back-248-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-248) The policy was later extended, with export bans on unprocessed copper, tin, bauxite and gold ores expected to take effect in mid-2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-249-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-249) By 2023, [Indonesian nickel exports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel_production_in_Indonesia "Nickel production in Indonesia") had risen from US$3 billion annually to US$30 billion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-250-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-250)

### Foreign policy

See also: [List of international presidential trips made by Joko Widodo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_international_presidential_trips_made_by_Joko_Widodo "List of international presidential trips made by Joko Widodo")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/60/P20221114AS-0411_%2852650920511%29.jpg/500px-P20221114AS-0411_%2852650920511%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:P20221114AS-0411_(52650920511).jpg) Joko Widodo and US President [Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden "Joe Biden") at the [2022 G20 Bali summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_G20_Bali_summit "2022 G20 Bali summit"), 14 November 2022

[![Widodo-Zelenskyy](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Meeting_of_the_President_of_Ukraine_with_the_President_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia_in_Kyiv_%285%29.jpg/500px-Meeting_of_the_President_of_Ukraine_with_the_President_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia_in_Kyiv_%285%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Meeting_of_the_President_of_Ukraine_with_the_President_of_the_Republic_of_Indonesia_in_Kyiv_(5).jpg)

[![Widodo-Putin](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/Vladimir_Putin_and_Joko_Widodo_%282022-06-30%29.jpg/500px-Vladimir_Putin_and_Joko_Widodo_%282022-06-30%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Vladimir_Putin_and_Joko_Widodo_(2022-06-30).jpg)

Joko Widodo meeting with Ukrainian President [Volodymyr Zelenskyy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volodymyr_Zelenskyy "Volodymyr Zelenskyy") in Kyiv on 29 June 2022 (top) and with Russian President [Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Putin "Vladimir Putin") in Moscow on 30 June 2022 (bottom)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/Jokowi_Xi_Jinping_2015.jpg/500px-Jokowi_Xi_Jinping_2015.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Jokowi_Xi_Jinping_2015.jpg) Indonesian President Joko Widodo meeting Chinese President [Xi Jinping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping "Xi Jinping")

Before Jokowi's election, Indonesia's foreign policy was shaped by his predecessor's dictum of "a thousand friends and zero enemies."[&#8202;](#ref-back-251-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-251) Jokowi outlined a three-pronged foreign policy centred on maintaining Indonesia's sovereignty, improving protection for Indonesian citizens, and intensifying economic diplomacy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-sovereignty-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-sovereignty) Indonesian foreign policy during his tenure has been described as "pragmatic and inward-looking", with a strong emphasis on economic development and investment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-253-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-253)[&#8202;](#ref-back-jakpostimpactful-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-jakpostimpactful) During his ten years in office, he did not attend a session of the [United Nations General Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly "United Nations General Assembly"), although he addressed it virtually in 2020 and 2021.[&#8202;](#ref-back-jakpostimpactful-1)[\[254\]](#refnote-jakpostimpactful)

Jokowi aspired to make Indonesia a global maritime power (_poros maritim dunia_, lit.'global maritime axis'). He saw the sea as central to Indonesia's future and argued that, as a maritime nation, Indonesia should assert itself between the [Indian Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean "Indian Ocean") and the [Pacific Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Ocean "Pacific Ocean"). The doctrine's five pillars were the revival of Indonesia's maritime culture, the protection and management of marine resources, the development of maritime infrastructure, connectivity, shipping and tourism, greater international maritime cooperation and conflict resolution at sea, and the strengthening of maritime defence forces.[&#8202;](#ref-back-255-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-255) As part of this vision, Jokowi adopted a tougher stance on [illegal fishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_fishing "Illegal fishing").[&#8202;](#ref-back-256-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-256) He said that Jakarta could no longer tolerate a situation in which more than 5,000 vessels were operating illegally in Indonesian waters each day, making a mockery of Indonesian sovereignty and causing annual losses of more than $20 billion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-257-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-257)[&#8202;](#ref-back-258-0)[\[258\]](#refnote-258)
